=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
January 19, 1940.
After receipt of the letter of Decem-
ber 14, 1939, from Mr. Basil Harris, Assistant
to the Secretary of the Treasury, to the Chief
of the Division of Far Eastern Affairs in re-
gard to instructions concerning treatment of
Japanese public vessels while in Hawaii, Mr.
Ballantine and Mr. Ward discussed the suhject
and Mr. Ward telephoned in the matter to
Mr. Harris. As a consequence of that conver-
sation, the Treasury Department took the
matter up with the Department of Commerce.
994.3501/62
The letter herewith (i08) of January 6
1940, from the Acting Secretary of Commerce
to the Secretary of State and its attachment
indicate the consequent action which was taken
by the Department of Commerce. Mr. Ward has
now telephoned to Mr. Harris referring to the
January 6 letter from the Acting Secretary of
Commerce, and stating that because the action
taken by the Department of Commerce appears
satisfactory no further action will be taken
by us.
No further action by the Department of
State would appear to be necessary. The cor-
respondence can therefore be filed.
5
JAN 2 4 1940

Dear Mr. Hamilton:
You are undoubtedly familiar with an "incident")at the
Sau FE.,
subport of Hilo, Hawaii, between a customs officer and a sentry
manno of
of the Japanese Coast Defense ship YAkUmO on October 24, 1939.
As a result of this "incident", the Collector of Customs at
1/19/40
Honolulu, Mr. J. Walter Doyle, sent the attached copy of a letter
894.3311/605
9.ω.
to the Honorable B. Kudo, Acting Consul-General or Japan, Honolulu,
894.3311
T. H., giving instructions issued to the Deputy Collector of Customs
at the port of Hilo, pertaining to the visit of the Japanese
Transport SHIRIYA while in the port of Hilo from November 24, to
November 28, 1939, and to the Japanese Transport IR0 while 1n the
port of Hilo from January 12, to January 16, 1940. These instruc-
tions seem to bequite thorough, and I am only wondering whether
there might be something in these instructions which might be
contrary or at variance to some attitude held by your Department,
and therefore I would appreciate very much your comments in this
matter.
In the event you have not seen the complete file on the
subjoct of the original "incident", I will be glad to send this
file over to you if you will just let me know.
Sincerely yours,
Banil hanis
Basil Harris
Assistant to the Seoretary.
JAN 2 6 1940
FTLED
Hon. Maxwell M. Hamilton,
Chief, Division of Far Eastern Affairs,
Department of State,
Washington, D. C.

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.99)
COPY
TREASURY DEPARTMENT
UNITED STATES CUSTOMS SERVICE
Honolulu, Hawaii
Office of the Collector
November 6, 1939.
Honorable B. Kudo,
Acting Consul-General of Japan,
Honolulu, T. H.
Sir:
Under date of October 17, 1939, the office of The Secretary of the Treasury has
authorized that the usual Customs courtesies and facilities be extended to the
Japanese Transport SHIRIYA while in the port of Hilo from November 24 to
November 28, 1939 and to the Japanese Transport Iro while in the Port of Hilo
from January 12 to January 16, 1940.
The Department further advises that the above named transports are calling at
the Port of Hilo for the purpose of replenishing their supply of water and food.
You are advised that the following instructions have been issued to the Deputy
Collector of Customs at the Port of Hilo relative to Customs control of the
above vessels while in that port:
1. Customs Guards are to be posted at the foot of the gangway.
2.
The Customs officer designated to extend the courtesies authorized
to the officer in command of the vessel shall be the first person
to board the vessel.
3.
The vessel may take on board only food and water or other essential
ship supplies necessary for the welfare of the vessel.
4.
No cargo of any kind, whether received by donation or otherwise shall
be ladened on board.
5. No armed guard is to be posted on the pier by the vessel at any time.
6.
Any plans originated by a reception committee or other person for the
entertainment of the personnel of the vessel in so far as such plans
may affect the movement of people on or around the vicinity of the
vessel while berthed at the pier must be submitted to the Deputy
Collector for his approval and after approval must be strictly adhered
to by all persons.
7.
That no other person or persons except properly designated Customs
officers shall have any authority on the piers in the vicinity where
the vessel is berthed concerning control thereof.

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
8.
That only articles of little or no value may be landed by any of
the personnel of the vessel or other person without the payment of
duty at the discretion of the Customs. All packages and articles
are to be opened for examination by Customs officers.
9.
That in the event conditions warrant, the Deputy Collector may, in
his discretion, stop all people from passing such Customs lines as
he may deem necessary to establish around the vessel under penalties
provided by law, except the Consul-General of Japan or his designated
representative producing proper credentials.
Respectfully,
/8/ J. WALTER DOYLE
Collector of Customs.

Dear Sir:
Mrs. Buford, of your office, called the Bureau with
reference to Bureau Memorandum No. 258, which wes jssued
as a result of the reports advising that public vessels
of Japan had taken on cargo in ports of Hawaii, and had
failed to obtain a clearance from tne Collector of
Customs at the ports of departure.
In a letter deted November 15, 1939, this Department
advised the Secretary of State that appropriate in-
structions would be issued to its field offices to the
effect that forein public vessels, attempting to take
on or discharge cargo while in our ports without
complying witn the provisions of Section 4197, R.S.,
as amended, and other applicable laws or regulations,
would have this statute brought to the attention of
the commander of such vessels, and that the commander
would be informed that full compliance with such
regulations would be mandatory.
A copy of Bureau Menorandum 258 is enclosed herevith.
Sincerely yours,
fMaus.fours

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPALTENT OF CO PERCE
Bureau of
Marine Inspection and Navigation
WASHINGTON
November 10, 1939
BUREAU MEM ORAUDU: NO. 258. (For distributicn to Bureau employees only.)
To:
Collectors of Customs and Deputy Collectors of Customs.
Subject: Foreign public vessels.
It has been brought to the attention of this Tureau that
foreign public vessels, to vhich the usual customs courtesies
and facilities of the ports have been extended, are taking
aboard scra: iron and other cor o for discharre at a sub-
sequent foreign port.
This Bureau is of the opinion that the customs ccurtesies
extended to foreign public vessels clearly do nct warrant
such vessels taking on bonrd miccellaneous cargo without
compliance with the provisions of Section l1?7, R. S., as
amended, and that when such vessels take abonrd miscellaneous
cargo destined for a forein port without complyin, with the
provisions of Section L197, P. S., as amended, such action
constitutes a clear abuse of the customs courtcsies extended
to such vessels, and should not be pernitted.
Therefore, if any forcign public versel at emuts to take
on cargo for discharge at a foreign port, withcut comply-
ing with the provisions of Section l197, R. S., or any
other applicable law or regulation, the com ander of such
vessel should be informed that full complinnce with such
requirements is mandatory.
If such nctification should be disregarded by the commander
of any foreign puhlic vessel, information to that effect
should be transmitted to this Bureau immediately for ap-.
propriate attention.
ed
R. S. Field,
Director.

February 5, 1943. Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
QUESTION OF WITHHOLDING FROM JAPANESE PUBLIC
STATE
 VESSELS PERMISSION TO VISIT PORTS IN THE
JUN1-1943
C
HAWAIIAN ISLANDS OTHER THAN HONOLULU
Jite
:0
SS
Dy
894.3311/610
Theavy Department 1n a letter dated February 5,
1940 recommended that when visits to the Hawai1an area
by Japanese public vessels were proposed by the Japanese
they be informed that such visits would be inconvenient
except atHonolulu.
The Department in a reply of February 21 referred to
a dec1sion in November 1937 by an inter-Departmental com-
mittee meeting to make a recommendation to the President
as follows: "The State Department to consider in consul-
tation with the Navy Department withholding from Japanese
public vessels permission to visi ports in the Hawaiian
Islands other than Honolulu." The Department also pointed
out that the recommendation of the Navy Department if
FW
affirmatively acted upon at that time might adversely
affect the relations between our naval forces in China
894.3311/6
waters and the Japanese Navy and might be prejudicial to
the efforts of our naval forces to render protection to
American interests in China during the continuance of hos-
tilities then going on in so far as such relations and such
efforts were dependent upon the good-will of the Japanese
Navy. The Department, while expressing the opinion that
1t would not seem to be an opportune time to put into
effect the recommendation under consideration, added that
should the Navy Department still consider that in the inter-
ests of national defense its recommendation should be
adopted, this Department would be disposed to agree in
principle provided that it was made applicable to the gov-
ernment vessels of all other countries.
On June 28, 1940, General Marshall, Chief of Staff, in
a meeting with Admiral Stark and Mr. Welles, recommended
that permission be denied to foreign vessels to visit ports
in the Territory of Hawai1 other than Honolulu. In identical
P
letters addressed on July 5, 1940 to the War and Navy Depart-
S/JT8
ments, the Department made reference to the recommendation
of General Marshall, concurred in by Admiral Stark, and
stated that when presented with further requests from foreign
governments for permission for their public vessels to visit
FILED
ports
-1943

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.95)
-2-
ports in the Territory of Hawaii other than Honolulu it
would reply that such visits were inconvenient. The
Department expressed doubt, however, whether it wculd be
practicable to close open ports in Hawaii to foreign
merchant vessels and suggested that the wide police powers
accorded to the United States Customs authorities by
certain Presidential proclamations might afford oppor-
tunity to institute routine safeguards which would
seriously hinder propaganda activities such as might be
undertaken by merchant vessels visiting those ports.

With reference to PR's request for an
indication of whether FE concurs in Navy's
recommendation that "when visits to the
Hawaiian area are proposed by the Japanese,
they be informed that such visits are incon-
venient except at Honolulu", FE assumes that
this recommendation refers to all Japanese
Government vessels.
This subject was discussed at an inter-
Departmental committee meeting in November
1937 when it was decided to make a recom-
mendation to the President as follows: "the
State Department to consider, in consultation
with the Navy Department, withholding from
Japanese public vessels permission to visit
ports in the Hawaiian Islands other than
Honolulu."
FE has noted the account given in the
enclosure to the letter of the Commandant of
the Fourteenth Naval District of December 1,
1939, of certain activities and incidents
in connection with visits of Japanese vessels
to the Hawaiian area. It is stated that these
visits are utilized to strengthen the rela-
tions of Japanese residents in Hawaii with
Japan

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
Japan and to encourage Japanese residents
to make donations of funds to Japan. It is
also stated that, although no special in-
telligence activities in connection with
these visits have been noted other than ex-
tensive photographing and the establishment
of contacts with Japanese known to be en-
gaged in activities in support of the Japanese
Government, the visits afford Japanese agents
excellent opportunities for carrying on their
work. Certain of the matters mentioned in the
file appear to have no bearing upon the
question presented by the Navy Department for
consideration. With regard to the incident
of the stationing of armed Japanese sentries
on the dock at Hilo, it is believed that in-
structions which have been issued to customs
authorities in Hawaii will prevent the recur-
rence of such an incident in the future. With
reference to the announcement made by the
Collector of Customs of regulations to be
enforced during future visits of foreign
public vessels (see numbered paragraph 4 of
the letter from the Commandant, Fourteenth
Naval District) it should be noted that these
regulations have been modified specifically
with reference to item 4 in pursuance of
telegraphic instructions issued on January 6,
1940, by the Acting Secretary of Commerce.
FE is of the opinion that the recommenda-
tions of the Navy Department, if affirmatively
acted

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
acted upon at the present time, might adversely
affect the relations between our naval forces
in China watera and the Japanese Navy, and
might be prejudicial to the efforts of our
naval forces to render protection to American
interests in China during the continuance of
the current hostilities in so far as such
relations are dependent upon the good-will of
the Japanese Navy.
Should the Navy Denartment still consider
that, in the interest of national defense, its
recommendation should be adopted, FE would be
disposed to agree in principle provided that
it was made aoplicable to the government
vessels of all foreign countries.

Acknowledgment is made of State Departmenthote of

894.3311/641
ports.
Confirming telephone conversation of July 24, 1940,
the Navy Department has no objection to the proposed visit

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
October 9, 1940
PA/
Mre mornbeck:
It is understood that inquiry has been
made of you as to the position of the
American Government with regard to re-
quested approval of visits to American
ports of the Japanese naval vessels
Naruto, Tsurumi and Kyokuto Maru.
T has informally discussed the mat-
ter with the Navy Department and has in-
formally been given an expression of view
on the part of the Navy Department that,
although there is reluctance to intrude
into a province properly the Department
of State's, it is undesirable that the
visits to Honolulu (Naruto November 2 and
Tsurumi November 16) should be approved,
because visits there might make possible
observations of movements of the United
States Navy. It appears that the Navy
Department is not particularly concerned
over visits of Japanese naval vessels to
California ports.
The visits to Los Angeles are stated
to be for the purpose of taking on crude
petroleum. It would appear inadvisable
at the present time to withhold approval
of the visits requested for the taking on
of oil because such withholding might be
interpreted

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
interpreted as being for the purpose of
placing a practical embargo upon the sup-
plying of oil to the Japanese Navy.
It is therefore suggested that the
Japanese Embassy be informed of approval
of visits of the three ships to Los
Angeles in accordance with the attached
draft note, but that there be no approval
of visits of these ships to Honolulu. It
is further suggested that Mr. Hornbeck's
reply to the inquiry mentioned in the
first paragraph above be along the same
lines.
mm.ll
894.3311/645
CC
FE:Coville:MHP

Mr. Martatra,tsttunselor of the Japanese
Embassy, left the attached document on
October 22, 1940 with Mr. Hamilton.
Mr. Morishima stated that he was not leaving
6
the document as a formal communication.
4
Mr. Marishima and Mir. Hamilton discussed
sels to American ports and it was indicated t&
Mr. Marishima that the Department has gone to
considerable lengths in attempting to accom-
modate itself to the naval vessel visits pro-
9.
posed by the Japanese, although such visits
to Hawaii under existing conditions cannot be
98
approved. Mr. Morishima stated that in the
instance of the Shiriya, the Japanese Government
would prefer that the visit be at Los ngeles
rather than at San Francisco. ir. Hamilton
indioated that there has been no decision on
the part of the nerican Government to disap-
prove all applications for  visits at Los
Angeles in favor of San Francisco, and that if
the Japanese Government desires that the
Shiriya visit Los Angeles instead of San Fran-
cisco, a communication to that effect should
F
be submitted to the Department and it will
be given consideration.
3
Mr. Hamilton called Mr. Marishima's atten-
tion to the fact that Japanese reguests for
approval of visits have frequently been on too
short notice, thereby complicating the question
of consideration of the requests. Mr. Marishima
stated that he would see to it that requests
come to the Department in plenty of time.
3m
FE:COVIIle:OJL

=== Page 194 === (confidence 0.99)
RECEIVED
1940
It has been the custom of the Imperial Japa-
nese Navy from time to time to dispatch naval
transports and supply ships to various ports in
FW
the United States, its territories and island
possessions. Hitherto these transports have been
894.33
admitted to these ports as a matter of course and
allowed to stay as long as was reasonably neces-
sary.
Lately, however, certain requests for admis-
9
sion of naval transports to the ports of Honolulu
5
and Los Angeles have been denied. In other cases
the period of stay has been sharply restricted,
much to the inconvenience of the complements, who
will be compelled to rush their work at inordinate
speed. Time allowed for necessary routines such
as loading supplies of water and provisions will
FLLED
be definitely insufficient.
It is needless to say that placing of such
restrictions on the movement of Japanese naval
transports contrasts so glaringly with past readi-
894.3311
B
ness on the part of the United States authorities
to supply different facilities to them that it can

=== Page 195 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
not fail to impress the Imperial Japanese Navy, which
is directly concerned.
In times like these it is needless to say that
anything that may affect unfavorably Japanese-Amerioan
relations should be avoided as far as possible.
Such being the case, I wish to place this matter
before you in the hope that the decisions of the
authorities concerned with regard to calls by Japa-
nese naval transports be modified so as to give their
agreement to the schedules proposed by the Japanese
naval authorities.

=== Page 311 === (confidence 0.98)
NAVY DEPARTMENT

CONFIDENTIAL
April 3, 1941
sir:
Reference is made to State Department letter dated
March 31, 194l, regarding a proposed visit of the Japanese
Naval Transport KYOKUTO MARU to San Franois∞ from April 5,
to 8, 1941, for the purpose of obtaining fuel o11. In
stating that the Navy Department has no objeotion to this
visit, the Secretary of the Navy desires to call attention
to the recent frequency of naval visits. Including an addi-
tional ship which has been reported confidentially, by the
American Oonsul at Kobe, as enroute to Los Angeles, but
regarding which no offioial request has yet been rsuaived, a
total of twelve Japanese naval vessels will have obtained a
cargo of oil from the United States within a period of six
months, five of these within a period of sixty days.
Certain of the vessels now listed as maval vessels have
made previous similar trips in their origimal status as com-
meroial vessels. It appears more than probable that their
current naval status has been devised to bestow upon them,
and upon their obvious purposes,a degree of mmmity which a
commercial vessel could scarcely command.
It is suggested that the recent frequenoy of suoh visits
is, to say the least, umusual; and a matter regarding which
it is considered that some restrietive polioy would be a time-
ly precaution to prevent the abuse of intermational courtesy
in a manner which appears contrary to the best interes's of
the United States.
Respectfully,
(s) Frank Knox
Frank Knox
The Honorable,
The Seeretary of State

Reference 1s made to State Department letter FE 894.
3311/685 dated Llay 17, 1941, enclosing for comment a copy
of a proposed communication to the Japanese Embassy with
regard to visits of Japanese special neval transports to
the Unitea Stetes.
894.3311/68
Since receipt of the foregoing reference it is the
understanding of this department that it has now been agreed
that these ships shall enter nort as merchant vessels sub-
Ject to the usual regulations of other departments of the
government and therefore not involving the courtesies due
a bona fide man-of-war. As has been stated before ships
in the status of a merchant-marine do not properly come
under the cognizance of the Nevy Department and permission
for such visits is unnecessary. However, inasmuch as there
are certain neval personnel aboard, the Nevy Department would
8
appreciate the courtesy or advance information regarding
6
such visits to ether wiin a list or the names of the naval
personnel eciually on ooard.
With regaru to a recent memorandum from the Japanese
3
Embassy to the Stete Depertment, regarding the status of
Japenese naval special transports, copy of which was fur-
nished to tne Navy Department, no exception is taken to the
fact that these special transports are opereted in behalf of,
and under the supervision of the Jepanese Navy. However,
the Navy Department reiterates its statement that the pre-
sence of e naval insnector, with e few attendant navel per-
sonnel, cannot imbue a commercial cargo vessel with,a char-
acter which entitles her to the international privileges and
immunities of a pona-fide men-of-war. The Navy Department
also reiterates its statement that such courtesies will be
accorded with pleasure to eny properly manned and recognized
man-of-war of the Japanese Navy.
In the case of a bona fide man-of-war, the Navy De-
partment considere it highly essential, and no more than
U

DEPARTMENT OF STATEPPOTOC
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Apr11 14, 1941
Consideration of the attached San
Francisco Chronicle article of April 4,
194l entitled "U. S. Oil Is Ferried to
Japan. Tankers Loading Millions of
Gailons Here", to which attention has
been called in connection with PR draft
communications in the matter of the pro-
posed visit of the Japanese naval trans-
port Erimo to Los Angeles from April 22
to April 25 for the purpose of taking on
crude petroleum, would appear to raise
two principal questions. One question
has to do with further control of export
of petroleum products, which subject is
currently under intensive study by the
Department. The second question has to
do with determining what are the exemp-
tions which Japanese naval trensports
as public vessels are enjoying on the
occasions of their visits to Pacific
ports in the United States to take on
crude petroleum, which would not he
enjoyed by commercial tankers.
R
The question of the exemptios 
joyed by Japanese naval transporta he
been and is under discussion betw en E,
PR and IN  and full information it the
matter wl be obtained as soon as
practicable. The Commissioner of Customs
has
894.3311

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.96)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
has indicated to IN that full details
cannot be positively known without com-
municating with San Francisco.
In the circumstances it would
appear inadvisable to hold up the PR
draft communications with regerd to the
Brimo visit inasmuch as the Erimo is
already on the high seas. The necessary
information for arriving at a general
policy with regard to the visit of Japa-
nese naval transports to United States
ports is expected to be available before
the visit of the Japanese naval transport
next after the Erimo, and possibly it
will be available before the Department
makes final reply to the Japanese Embassy
with regard to the visit of the Erimo.
(It should be noted that the attached PR
draft communications are preliminary com-
municatione in the matter of the Erimo,
and their sending would not close the
question.)

The Japanese Ambassador presents his complimeats
to the Honorable the Secretary of State and has the
honor to acknowledge the receipt of and to express
6
his thanks for the Secretary's three notes, all dated
June 3, 194l, in regard to the Japanese naval special
transports KOKUYO MARU, TOEI MARU and HOYO MARU.
In compliance with the desire of the United
PS/LB
States Government, and by way of establishing for the
vessels in question the full status of public naval
6:
vessels, the Ambassador takes pleasure in giving infor-
mation hereunder in regard to the status of the vessels
in question, as provided for by the Japanese Naval
Regulations.
"Japanese naval special transports are
those vessels which have been requisitioned
under the necessity of the Japanese Navy and
engage in transportation of necessary com-
modities for the Japanese Navy.
"For this reason the Japanese Navy
places on board each vessel one inspeoting
naval

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
naval officer, a few officers and a rumber of
warrant officers and enlisted men. The inspect-
ing officer is authorized to order and command
the captain of the ship in carrying out an order
or orders given by the Japanese Navy Department.
"These vessels belong to the Japanese Navy
and are mentioned in the Japanese naval ships'
list."
The Anbassador has further the honor to add that,
as will be seen by his communications of the 5th and 7th
of February last, the vessels under consideration visited
San Francisco previously under exactly the same status as
at present.
June 5, 1941
X

PR
The Governor of California has telegraphed
asking whether the taking on of crude
petroleum by the Japanese naval transport
hiriya is now (following the "freezing order")
permissible. The Navy Department 1s also mak-
ing inquiry in regard to this matter in order
that the Naval Commandant concerned may be
informed of the decision reached.
On July 22 the Japanese Embassy requested
permission for the Japanese naval transport
Shiriva to visit Los Angeles between August 9
and August 14 for the purpose of taking on
orude petroleum. On July 25 the Department
notified the interested branches of the Federal
Government and the Governor of California in
the usual way. The Navy Department, in an un-
dated letter received in the Department on
July 30, has stated that no objeotion to the
ontrance of the Shiriya is perceived and the
landing is approved. It is understood that the
Department does not necessarily await the re-
ceipt of replies from the other interested
authorities before taking action in cases of
this sort. This Government's agreement to the
proposed visit has not been conveyed to the
Japanese Embassy.
FE recommends

=== Page 372 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
T recommends that reply be made to the
Japanese Embassy stating that no objection
is pereeived to the proposed visit of the
Shiriya. At the same time the Japanese Embassy's
attention should be direoted to the fact that,
in addition to the licenses which normally will
be required for certain exports under the export
control measures of this Government, with
respeot to all exports from this country specifio
and individual licenses would have to be applied
for at the Treasury Department which would reach
a decision in each case in accordance with the
polioies of this Government and upon the merits
of the individual application.
FE further recommends that a reply be sent
to the Governor of California stating that per-
mission has been granted for the entry of the
vessel and that with respect to the taking on
of orude oil the attention of the Japanese Em-
bassy has been thy to the fact that specific
and individual'licenses for all exports from
this country in addition to normal license re-
quirements under the export control measures,
would have to be applied for at the Treasury
Department, which would reach a decision in each
case in accordance with the policles of this
Government and upon the merits of the individual
application.

What are the exemptions (and what is the extent of
such exemptions) which Japanese naval transports as public
894.3311/706
vessels are currently accorded on the occasions of their
visits to Facific ports of the United States to take on
crude petroleum, which would not be accorded to commercial
tankers?
Are the allegations contained in an article in the
San Francisco Chronicle, April 4, 1911 entitled "U. S.
Oil is Ferried to Japan; Tankers Loading Millions of
Gallons Here", confirmed by the facts? In what amounts
would fees and taxes have been payable by the vessels if
they were not public vessels? The principle allegatfons
of the newspaper article are as follows:
AUG
1. No naval transports pay port charges.
2. There is practically no Federal supervision or
PS
inspection of them.
/RJH
3. Public vessels enjoy immunity from Customs beyond
the filing of a manifest.
4. Custom officers do not set foot on such vessels for
any purpose.
5. No entry or claims contract is made with the Customs.
6. Japanese maval transports escape all fees and tonnage
taxes at American ports, which in the case of vessels engaged
in
6
70

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.99)

DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
in general cargo operations total many thousands of dollars.
7. The Japanese Consulate files a manifest but the
cargo is not checked against the manifest.
8. Eight times more oil leaves Estero Bay and Port
San Luis in Japanese tankers than leaves San Francisco in
Japanese tankers.

Department of State
Apr11 18, 1941
In reply to the memorandum prepared by the Division
894.33
of Far Eastern Affairs dated April 14, 194l, regarding
the 1mmunities enjoyed by Japanese naval tankers (1.e.
public vessels), the following comments may be pertinent:
Facts would appear to indicate that a substantial
/707
quantity of crude o1l and some gasoline 1s being loaded
upon Japanese naval tankers at Paoifio coast ports of
the United States. The exaot amount 1s not readily to
be asoertained but is believed in most inatanoes to be a
matter of record in the offices of the Coast Guard. In-
quiries regarding the volume of petroleum produots car-
ried on these vessels should be aooompanied by specifio
names of vessels and approximate dates of arrival or
P
S
departure. Inquiries should be addressed to Commander W. N.
R
89
4.3311
Derby, Chief, Maritime Service, United States Coast Guard.
JH
Ordinary Japanese commercial tankers pay entrance
and olearanoe fees as well as tonnage dues based on
AUG1-1941
rates provided in a proolamation of President Grant in
AILED
1871. For example: the ship Toe1 Maru, a Japanese
tanker of 5820 net tons,1s required to pay entrance fees
of $2.50 and olearance fees of $2.50 each t1me she
calls at a United States port (see Sec. 2654, Revised
Statutes). If she is in service between some Japanese
707
port

=== Page 390 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
port and oalls at only one United States port to load
her oargo of petroleum produots, she is assessed s1x
cents per net ton on each voyage (not to exceed five
voyages or 30 cents per year) (Sec. 4219, Revised
Statutes). Thus, total port charges would amount to
$354.20 ($5.00 plus $349.20) per visit. Should Japanese
commeroial tankers not enjoy the benefits provided in
the President's proclamation of 1871 total charges for
this vessel would amount to some $6,174.20 per visit.
Japanese public vessels pay no charges or fees.
The allegations made by the San Franoisco Chronicle
on April 4, 194l and summarized in the memorandum of
April 14 may be informally answered as follows:
(1) Naval transports as publio vessels are not re-
quired to file an entry certificate at United States
ports if not engaged in commercial activities. When
no entry certificate is required then no tonnage dues
are assessed against a vessel (Title 19, U.S.0. 1441).
(2) Generally speaking it is true that there is
practically no federal supervision for inspeotion of
foreign publio vessels calling at United States ports.
Customs collectors and their subordinate officers when
acting as agents of the United States Oustoms Service
would not ordinarily board a foreign publio vessel unless
they had reason to believe that such vessel was entering
an

=== Page 391 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
an Amerioan port in violation of customs regulations re-
garding the importation of opium, et cetera. Customs
colleotors and their subordinate offioers may, however,
board foreign public vessels as agents for the Departments
of State or Commerce, but in view of the immunity of for-
eign public vessels from filing entry certificates, it 1s
unlikely that oustoms officers would have occasion to
board these vessels in the interests of the Department of
Commerce.
(3) The immunity from customs regulations enjoyed
by foreign public vessels 1s outlined above. If a mani-
fest is requested it 1s probable that such request orig1-
nates informally from the Office of the Collector of
Customs at the port of call.
(4) The matter of customs officers boarding foreign
public vessels is outlined above.
(5) See paragraph no. 1.
(6) Please refer to the third paragraph of this
memorandum in whion speoific fees and charges are listed.
(7) Ne1ther the Customs Service nor the Coast Guard
nor the Department of Commerce is able to confirm or
deny the report that the Japanese Consulate files man1-
fests for visiting Japanese publio vessels.
(8) No exaot statistics regarding shipments of
orude

=== Page 392 === (confidence 0.94)
-4
crude oil and other petroleum products in foreign publio
vessels are available for specific ports on the Pacifio
Ocean. It 1s probable that shipments from port San Luis
are considerably heavier than those from San Franoisco.
For further information inquiries should be addressed to
the Coast Guard, as indicated in the second paragraph of
this memorandum.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
May 14, 1941
The reason for suggesting the referring
to the Navy Department of the draft note to
the Japanese Embaesy in the matter of visits
of naval transports is that, from informal
conversations with different officers in the
Navy Department, I gather that opinion 1s
not unanimous and that they may be beginning
to wonder whether there might be complications
1f the Japanese should reciprocally request
us for six months' notice of visits of American
naval vessels to Japan. My guess is that we
will not find the Navy inclined to take any
stronger action in the matter of visits of
Japanese naval transports to California,
and that they may not feel inclined to do
even as much as is proposed in the draft note.
Because of differences of view within the
Navy I see no appropriate way of clearing
up the question except by referring the draft
note to the Navy.

on of

Depes tmant of State
In the matter of the draft note to the
Japanese Embassy requesting a six months
approximate schedule of visits to American
ports of Japanese naval transports, the Navy
Department has informally exoressed the view
that the supplying of such schedule should not
be construed as a blanket request for apnroval
of the visits listed but that the usual request
for permission for each visit should be made
in each individual instance, in addition to
the schedule to be supplied in advance. PR
has accordingly revised the last paragraph of
the draft note to the Japanese Embassy.
Although the draft note has received the
informal approval of the concerned office
of the Navy, it is suggested that it be sent
by letter to the Navy for consideration and
for comment#. A draft letter to Navy is
attaohed; and it is suggested that the draft
note to the Japanese Embassy be held in
abeyance pending the Navy's reply.

MYHORNBECK
MAY 8- 1941
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Divisiokbr
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFA
PROTOCOL
Apr11 30, 1941
MAY1090
It is noted that the Navy Department, in recently
informing the Department of State that it has no objection
to certain proposed visits of Japanese naval transports to
take on crude petroleum in California ports, has called
894.33
attention to the frequency of such visits and has raised
for consideration the question whether their frequency
1s not an abuse of the usual courtesies and facilities
which are extended to the transports as public vessels.
17
Officers of the Navy Department have informelly stated,
0
8
in reply to inquiry as to what particular abuses t may
have in mind, that there are several respects in which
Japanese transportation of petroleum in public vessels
rather than in commercial tankers 1s inimical to American
interests: (1) the freedom of the public vessels from
policing while in American waters facilitates espionage,
PS/RJH
and persons engaged in such activities might easily be
taken on or dropped off; (2) the public vessel status of
the transports on the high seas simplifies their relation
to belligerents and exempts them from liability to search,
3311
as for example might take place if Japanese commeroial
tankers were believed by the British to be engaged in
ing produots destined for Germany; and (3) the spacing
regularity
1611

=== Page 396 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
regularity of the visits has resulted in the presence on
the Pacific coast of a Japanese transport most of the
time.
The Navy Department attaches no importance, and be-
lieves no importance should be attached, to the matter of
the exemptions enjoyed by the Japanese naval transports
from the payment of certain fees and cherges. (In an
article in the San Franciace Chronicle, April 4, 1941,
entitled "U. S. Oil Is Ferried to Japan, Tankers Loading
Millions of Gallons Here", it is alleged that the trans-
ports escape payments totaling "many thousands of dollars";
but the fact appears to be that it is customary in American
port cities for chambers of commerce and other representa-
tives of local business, as an attraction to shipping, to
arrange for relieving ships, commercial or otherwise, from
a considerable share of the charges which would fall upon
them.) IN states in memorandum of April 18, 1941 that the
total port charges on the Toei Maru, one of the Japanese
naval transports, 5820 net tons, would be $354.20 per
visit if assessed on the basis of a commercial ship.
It is learned from Co that export licenses are re-
quired for the shipping of petroleum products on Japanese
naval transports exaotly as if on commercial ships. On the
other hand, the Director of the Bureau of Marine Inspection
and

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
and Navigation in the Department of Commerce (Commander
Richard S. Field) has informed the Department that, although
manifest and clearance are required of public vessels if
they load general cargo for discharge at foreign ports, mani-
fest and clearance are not required from the Japanese naval
transports in connection with taking on crude petroleum,
in as much as such petroleum is considered to be naval sup-
plies.
According to IN's memorandum of April 18, 1941, there
is practically no federal supervision for inspection of
foreign public vessels calling at United States ports.
Customs collectors and their subordinate officers when
acting as agents of the United States Customs Service
would not ordinarily board a foreign public vessel unless
they had reason to believe that such vessel was entering
an American port in violation of customs regulations regard-
ing the importation of narcotics, et cetera. Customs
collectors and their subordinate officers may board foreign
public vessels as agents for the Departments of State or
Commerce, but in view of the immunity of foreign public
vessels from filing entry certificates, there is little
likelihood thet occasion will be found for customs offioers
to board such vessels on behalf of the Department of Com-
meroe.
The

=== Page 398 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
The point made by the Navy Department is that the
numerous visits of the Japanese naval transports give
rise to the question whether their frequency is not an
abuse of the usual courtesies and facilities which are
extended to them as public vessels. Presumably the ques-
tion at issue is not the guestion of taking action which
might limit the quantity of oil reaching Japan; it is to
be assumed that decision on policy in that regard would
be and is better arrived at in connection with decisions
in the enforcement of the export control act. Japanese
naval transports are not in position to transport any oil
the export of which is illegal under the export control
regulations. The American Government might readily refuse
approval of visits of Japanese naval transports in their
recent frequency, but it is not believed that this would
work any substantial delay in oil shipments to Japan. It
would appear likely that Japanese naval tankers could be
quickly transferred to commercial registry and call at
American ports for oil with little or no delay. It is
possible also that there might result a shift of Japanese
naval tankers from the American run to the Netherlands
Indies run, and of Japanese commercial tankers from the
Netherlands Indies to the American, and it is doubtful if
this shift would serve American interest.
There

=== Page 399 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
There remains the matter of the exemptions enjoyed
by the Japanese naval transports as public vessels. It
would appear that the primary concern of the Navy Depart-
ment in the matter derives from the advantage for
espionage and other irregular activities which the Japanese
naval tankers possess by reason of their status as public
vessels and by reason of the regular, long, and frequent
visits which they make to American ports with the exemp-
tions of public vessels. In the interest of guarding
against such espionage and other irregular activities,
the American Government might appropriately withhold ap-
proval of such frequent visits, simply on the ground that
they are not convenient.
It is suggested that the Japanese Government be in-
vited to present promptly an approximate schedule of pro-
posed visits of Japanese naval transports to American ports
during the next six months in order that it may be given
consideration by this Government. It is further suggested
that the communicating of this invitation be incorporated
in a reply (draft herewith) to the Japanese Embassy in the
matter of extension of courtesies and facilities to the
Japanese naval transport Erimo, and that in such reply
the attention of the Embassy be invited to the fact that
the Erimo arrived in San Francisco on April 21 in advance
of schedule, before approval had been expressed for it to
visit

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.89)
-6-
visit any American port.
After having received the schedule of visits which
we request of the Japanese Embassy, the Department in
consultation with the Navy can decide whether it would be
appropriate and desirable to indicate to the Japanese
Embassy that we desire to have any portion of the scheduled
visits canceled as inconvenient.

Department of State
June 4, 1941
Mr. Wogdward
I read the note drafted in FE to Commander McCracken.
He does not feel that we can say to the Embassy that the
Department will be glad to give consideration to reguests
for approval of proposed visits of such vessels to American
ports since cargo vessels may enter ports without obtain-
ing permission. He suggests that this note be held until
the comment made by the Navy Department on a draft which
the State Department sent over is received. He prefers
saying to the Embassy that since there is naval personnel
aboard, this Government would appreciate the courtesy of
being informed when such ships are expected to arrive and
the names of the naval personnel.
Commander McCracken also stated that he cannot recall
any other country having naval personnel aboard merchant
vessels.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DCR
Sir:
Reference is made to State Department letter PR
894.33
dated May 15, 1941 regarding a proposed visit of the Jap-
anese special naval transport KOKUYO MARU to Los Angeles
from May 27 to May 30, 1941, for the purpose of taking on
crude petroleum.
Although the matter was not pressed during the previous
visit of the KOKUYO MARU to San Francisco April 18, to April
21, the Navy Department is unable to identify the KOKUYA
MARU as a bona fide vessel of the Japanese Navy and it is
therefore in some doubt as to the interpretation of the
courtesies and facilities which are requested. When a bona
fide man-of-war visits a port it is courtesy and custom
that the name of the commanding officer be furnished. How-
ever the request in this case specifically states that the
senior officer on board is an inspector, and in no way indi-
cates that the ship is under his command. In view of this
unusual situation the Navy Department would appreciate some
amplification of the exact status of the ship. If she is
merely an oil cargo ship aboard which has been placed a
naval inspector, the Navy Department fails to see any reason
why she should be accorded the privileges, immunities and
courtesies which would be accorded with pleasure to any
recognized ship of the Japanese Navy.
P
S
If, under these anomalous circumstances, the KOKUYO
/RJ
MARU enters Los Angeles as a merchant ship subject to all
applicable regulations the matter is beyond the cognizance
331
of the Navy Department. Should the Japanese Government insist
H
that the ship is entitled to the courtesies and privileges
of a bona-fide man-of-war, the Navy Department does not con-
sider such request as legitimate and recommends that in this
case, and all subsequent similar cases, the Japanese Govern-
ment be informed that the visit is not convenient.
AUG1-1941
Respectfully,
Foues tal
The Honorable,
The Secretary of State.
J
711

=== Page 417 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF PROTOCOL
May 26, 1941.
EASTERN AFFAIRS
1941
MEMORANDUM
FOR
THE
FILES
Department of State
Following consultation with FE and LE, this
Division telephoned to the Japanese Embassy and said
that if the Kokuyo Maru entered Los Angeles as a
commercial vessel on or about May 27, there would
no objection to the visit. If, on the other hand, she
was to be considered a Japanese naval unit, further in-
formation would have to be furnished by the Japanese
Embassy, concerning the nature of the vessel and its
visit, since our Navy Department was unable to identify
it as a bona fide naval vessel.
Subsequently the Embassy telephoned and said that
the Kokuyo Maru would enter Los Angeles as a commercial
vessel.
Stanley Woodward

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

June 2, 1941
It is understood that the attached
aide-memoire fro  the Japanese Embassy, dated
May 31, 194l, in the matter of the proposed
visit of the Japanese naval special transport
Hoyo Maru to California ports on June 3, 194l,
was left with Assistant Secretary Berle on
May 31 by the Counselor of the Japanese mbassy
who stated that the Japanese Imbassy hopes
that the American Government will accord the
courtesies and facilities of public vessels
to Japanese naval special transports and that
such decision will be made not later than June 2
in order that the Hoyo Maru, which is scheduled
to arrive on June 3, will be accorded such
courtesies and facilities.
It is suggested that the ounselor of the
Japanese Embassy be invited to call and that
an oral reply be made to him by an ofricer
of FE along iines as follows:
The Department has made note of the
information su plied by the Japanese Eimbassy
in the memorandum dated May 28, 1941, that
Japanese naval special transports are re-
quisitioned vessels, and that the ranking offier
of

=== Page 426 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
of the Japanese Navy on board is not the
captain of the vessel but is described as
an inspecting naval officer. American ports
are frequently visited by ships requisitioned
by various governments, and it is not the
practice of the American Government to accord
the caurtesies and facilities of public vessels
to such ships (nor have such courtesies and
facilities been requested by those governments),
except in the event that such requisitioned
vessels should be fully manned by naval crews.
The according of full public vessel immunities
to Japanese naval special transports would,
therefore, involve a new decision which would
affect the treatment to be accorded to ships
of a number of nationalities, and the Depart-
ment is not disposed at the present time to
revise its practice in this regard. This
Government, although unable to accord the
full status of public naval ves. elsto
Japanese naval special transports as described
in the Japanese Embassy's informal memorandum
of May 28, 1941, will be εlad to give considera-
tion to requests for approval of proposed
visits of such vessels to American ports without
the courtesies and facilities accorded to ships
in the full status of public naval vessels.

Mr. Okumura called at the Department at 6 p.m., having
6
telephoned a few minutes before that he desired to take up a
matter of some urgency. He referred to the Embassy's note
124, May 14, 194l in which inquiry was made whether it
would be agreeable to the United States Government if the
Japanese naval special transport Kokuyo Maru should visit
Los Angeles from May 27 to May 30 for the purpose of taking
on crude petroleum, and whether the vessel would be
accorded the usual courtesies and facilities. He stated
AUG 2 619
that the Embassy had fust received a telephone call from
the Japanese Consul at Los Angeles reporting that the
Kokuyo Maru arrived at Los Angeles on May 27 and had not
been

=== Page 436 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
been accorded the courtesies and facilities which had
been accorded similar transports whch have visited American
ports in recent months. Mr. Okumura asked that this be
brought to the attention of the appropriate divisions of
the Department with a view to according the vessel such
courtesies and facilities as before.
Ir. Coville stated that it was his understanding that
there had been communicated on May 24 by telephone to the
Japanese Embassy a message to the effect tnt further
infcrmation was desired by t: is Covernment if the Japanese
Government wished to e stablish for the Kokuyo Maru the
full status of a public naval vessel, but that, because of
the imminence of the date of t e proposed arrival at
Los Angeles (May 27), this Government would not object to
the arrival of the Kokuyo Maru without the immunities of a
public naval vessel.
Mr. Okumura acknowledged the receipt by the Embassy
of such a telephone communication, and added that in the
Embassy's view the arrival of the Kokuyo Maru under such
conditions did not preclude further consideration by the
American Government of the question of according the
courtesies and facilities which are granted to public
naval vessels, and that it was for the purpose of requesting
such

=== Page 437 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
such consideration that the Embassy was now bringing
up the matter.
Mr. Coville inquired whether the Embassy was in
position to support its claim for immunities for the
Kokuyo Maru by supplying data additional to that included
in the Embassy's note, which had not been found adequate
to establish for the ship a right to the full courtesies and
facilities accorded to public naval vessels. Mr. Okumura
stated that he would request of the appropriate Japanese
officials, for later supplying to the Department, further
pertinent information with regard to the public vessel
status of the Kokuyo Maru. Mr. Coville informed Mr. Okumura
that the fact of Mr. Okumura's having called would be
brought to the attention of the appropriate divisions of
the Dep artment.

ASSISTANT COMMANDANT'S OFFICE
ELEVENTH NAVAL DISTRICT
Pier "A", Long Beach, California
Contidential
July 14, 1941.
CONFIDENTIAL
From:
The Assistant Commandant.
To:
The Chief of Naval Operations.
Via:
The Commandant, Eleventh Naval District.
Subject:
Abuse of courtesies of the Port by Japanese
Naval vessels.
(A) ADIC, L.n., Confidential memo to AsstCom
Enclosure:
11 of July 10, 1941.
1.
In addition to photographing of building ways
and operations at the Bethlehem Steel Company Shiphuilding
Plant in San Fedro as set forth in enclcsure (A), certain
other minor abuses of the courtesies of the Fort by Japanese
Naval vessels have been noted.
2.
This same ship, the TRo, after anchoring in
the outer harbor, conducted pulling boat drills. One pulling
boat exercised at rowing covering the distance from her anchor-
age to a point directly in front of the Bethlehem shipbuilding
ways where the crew immediately executed "oars". They remained
there for sometime.
3.
On another occasion, on e prior visit of the
KYOKUYO MARU, and while she was a merchant vessel chartered
to the Imperial Japanese Navy, boat drills were again indulged
in. From her position in the explosive anchorage a small boat
was dispatched in an easterly direction to a distance of about
3,ooo yards where anti-submarine nets were in the process of
being anchored. After hovering in the vicinity of these nets,
the boat returned in a direct line to the KYOkUYO MARU and was
immediately hoisted on board. This was observed under glasses
from the Naval Air Station, Terminel Island, California.

=== Page 445 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
July 14, 1941.
Subject:
Abuse of courtesies of the Port by
Japanese Navel vessels.
4.
The AssistantCommandantis thoroughly aware
that such practices can be of but small consequence. They
can result in but an inappreciable addition to Japanese
Neval Intelligence. They are, however, irritating.
/S/ RICHARD B. COFFMAN

ONFIDENTIAL
July 10, 1941.
MEMO FOR:
Assistant Commandant, Eleventh Naval District,
Long Beach, California.
SUBJECT:
Japanese Naval Tanker "IRO", re: Thotography
of Los Angeles Shipbuilding & Drydock Company
by Members of Crew of.
1.
On June 9, 194l, Cuptain H. S. JEANS. U.S.
Navy (Ret.), Supervisor of Shiphuilding, Los Angeles Ship-
building and Drydock Corporetion, edvised this office that
members of the crew of subject vessel were photographing from
Shipboard the construct on end activitjes at above shipyard.
2.
The "IRo", which arrived in Los  ngeles Harbor
on the evening of July 8, 1941, was berthed at Fier 240-A,
General Petroleum Corporation docks, on July 9, l94l, and it
was from this berth that above descrihed photographing took
place.
3.
On the merning of July 10, 1941, the "IR0" was
shifted to an anchorage in the outer harbor, where she will
remain for two or three days.
4.
In view of the naval status of this vessel, no
action was taken by this office.
M. F. Heslip for
K. D. Ringle,
Lietenant Commander, U.S. Navy.
